Network-AI sandbox escape: node spawns /bin/sh -c with allowlist prefix + shell metacharacter injection AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the CVE-2026-54051 core exploit: the network-ai SandboxPolicy allowlist glob-matches the whole command string but ShellExecutor runs it via /bin/sh -c, so an allowed prefix (git/npm/node/yarn/pnpm) followed by ;, &&, ||, $() or backticks becomes arbitrary execution. Surfaces a node parent spawning a shell with -c where the command line carries both an allowlisted prefix and an unquoted shell metacharacter. Rationale: Uses the exact mechanism named in the article — node/npm/git/yarn/pnpm allowlist prefixes glob-matched whole-string, executed through /bin/sh -c, plus the article's enumerated metacharacter set (; & | $ ` ( ) < > { }). The node-parent + sh -c + prefix + metachar combination is the article's primary exploit (UC_12_2).
ATT&CKT1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate npm lifecycle scripts that chain shell commands with && (e.g. "build && test")
  • CI/CD runners (Jenkins, GitLab runner) invoking node tooling that shells out
  • pm2 / nodemon process managers launching shell wrappers
  • Developer running a node script that calls git with a $(...) subshell
  • Postinstall hooks combining multiple commands with ;
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm run","npm exec","yarn run","node_modules/.bin") and ProcessCommandLine has_any ("&&","||") and not ProcessCommandLine has_any ("id;","whoami","/tmp/","curl ","wget ","nc "))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full ProcessCommandLine and the parent node command line; identify whether the shell payload follows a legitimate allowlist prefix and whether the metacharacter introduces an unrelated command (id, curl, nc) vs a benign build chain. 2) Contain: if the host runs network-ai < 5.9.1 and the injected segment is non-build (recon/network), isolate the host and kill the orchestrator process; capture /proc and any dropped marker files. 3) Hunt: pivot on the same node parent for child recon binaries and outbound connections; check for network-ai version and remove broad wildcard allowlist entries (node */npm *).
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "node"
| where FileName in~ ("sh","bash","dash")
| where ProcessCommandLine has "-c"
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"\b(git|npm|node|yarn|pnpm)\b"
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"[;&|`$(){}<>]"
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has "node"
| where ActingProcessName has_any ("sh","bash","dash")
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "-c"
| where ActingProcessCommandLine matches regex @"\b(git|npm|node|yarn|pnpm)\b"
| where ActingProcessCommandLine matches regex @"[;&|`$(){}<>]"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Network-AI CVE-2026-54051 Allowlist Bypass via node Spawning Shell
id: 6f2c1b4a-8e3d-4c7a-9b21-0a5d7e9f1c33
description: node process spawning /bin/sh -c with an allowlisted command prefix and an unquoted shell metacharacter, matching the CVE-2026-54051 SandboxPolicy bypass.
references:
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-qw6v-5fcf-5666
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/20
tags:
  - attack.t1059.004
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: linux
detection:
  selection_parent:
    ParentImage|endswith: '/node'
  selection_child:
    Image|endswith:
      - '/sh'
      - '/bash'
      - '/dash'
  selection_prefix:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'git '
      - 'npm '
      - 'node '
      - 'yarn '
      - 'pnpm '
  selection_meta:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - ';'
      - '&&'
      - '||'
      - '$('
      - '`'
  condition: selection_parent and selection_child and selection_prefix and selection_meta
level: high
source:linux.auditd @auditd.type:EXECVE @process.name:(sh OR bash OR dash) @process.command_line:*-c* @process.command_line:(*git* OR *npm* OR *node* OR *yarn* OR *pnpm*)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2
| ParentBaseFileName=/^node$/i
| ImageFileName=/\/(sh|bash|dash)$/i
| CommandLine=/\s-c\s/
| regex("\\b(git|npm|node|yarn|pnpm)\\b", field=CommandLine)
| regex("[;&|`$(){}<>]", field=CommandLine)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())
ENDPOINT

| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| where match(process,"(?i)\b(git|npm|node|yarn|pnpm)\b") AND match(process,"[;&|`$(){}<>]")
| `security_content_ctime(firstTime)`
| `security_content_ctime(lastTime)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where Processes.parent_process_name=node (Processes.process_name=sh OR Processes.process_name=bash OR Processes.process_name=dash) Processes.process="*-c*" by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.parent_process_name Processes.process_name Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

Network-AI CVE-2026-54051 post-exploit: Linux recon utilities spawned from shell child of node AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects the post-exploitation enumeration that follows a successful CVE-2026-54051 allowlist bypass: a shell (sh/bash/dash) whose parent is node executing reconnaissance binaries. Fires when the orchestrator-spawned shell runs id, whoami, uname, hostname, curl, wget, nc, ncat, python, perl, ssh-keygen or cat. Rationale: Maps the article's post-exploitation profile (id ran via the injected /bin/sh -c in the PoC) and the reviewer-supplied recon binary set, scoped to a node grandparent in Defender/Falcon where InitiatingProcessParentFileName is available. Sentinel ImProcessCreate exposes only parent (sh) so the node grandparent constraint is relaxed there.
ATT&CKT1033 T1082
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Node-based deploy scripts legitimately calling curl/wget to fetch artifacts
  • Healthcheck wrappers running id/whoami/hostname inside containers
  • CI jobs invoking python via a shell step
  • Admin troubleshooting scripts under a node-managed service
  • Container entrypoint shells gathering uname/hostname at startup
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("/healthcheck","/entrypoint","deploy.sh") )
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the grandparent is the network-ai orchestrator node process and the recon command was not part of a known deploy/healthcheck script; review the preceding sh -c command line for an injected metacharacter. 2) Contain: if recon is paired with outbound nc/curl/wget to an untrusted host, isolate the device and snapshot the process tree and any written files. 3) Hunt: search the same host/account for the allowlist-bypass parent event (UC1), outbound C2, ssh-keygen output, and credential file reads; verify network-ai is upgraded to >= 5.9.1.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessParentFileName =~ "node"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("sh","bash","dash")
| where FileName in~ ("id","whoami","uname","hostname","curl","wget","nc","ncat","python","python3","perl","ssh-keygen","cat")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("sh","bash","dash")
| where ActingProcessName in~ ("id","whoami","uname","hostname","curl","wget","nc","ncat","python","python3","perl","ssh-keygen","cat")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Network-AI CVE-2026-54051 proof-of-concept artifact execution (poc-316.js / PWNED-316.txt) AIExploitation High
Hunts for execution or file activity referencing the published CVE-2026-54051 PoC artifacts — the poc-316.js exploit script and its PWNED-316.txt success marker. A direct, high-fidelity indicator of active testing or exploitation of the network-ai allowlist bypass. Rationale: Targets the literal exploit artifacts named in the article PoC (`node poc-316.js`, marker `PWNED-316.txt`). These strings are unique to CVE-2026-54051 testing, giving a very low-FP alerting signal across process and file telemetry.
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security team intentionally validating the patch by running the published PoC
  • A vulnerability scanner or red-team toolkit shipping poc-316.js
  • A file named poc-316.js committed to an internal research repo and opened by an editor
  • Mirror/backup process copying a saved copy of the advisory PoC
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm whether the host belongs to an authorised security/red-team tester; check the parent process (node) and whether PWNED-316.txt was actually written. 2) Contain: on a non-authorised host, isolate it, preserve poc-316.js and any marker file, and kill the network-ai orchestrator process. 3) Hunt: pivot to UC1/UC2 on the same host/account for the allowlist-bypass parent and post-exploit recon; confirm network-ai is upgraded to >= 5.9.1 and remove wildcard allowlist entries.
union
( DeviceProcessEvents
  | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
  | where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("poc-316.js","PWNED-316.txt")
  | project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, Source="Process", InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, Evidence=ProcessCommandLine ),
( DeviceFileEvents
  | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
  | where FileName in~ ("poc-316.js","PWNED-316.txt")
  | project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName=InitiatingProcessAccountName, Source="File", InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, Evidence=FolderPath )
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("poc-316.js","PWNED-316.txt")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Asset inventory: hosts running vulnerable network-ai (CVE-2026-54051, < 5.9.1) AIReconnaissance High
Foundational vulnerability-exposure hunt: enumerates hosts where the vulnerability-management feed reports CVE-2026-54051 (network-ai < 5.9.1). Used to scope which assets the behavioural detections (UC1–UC3) should be prioritised against and to drive patch/upgrade to 5.9.1. Rationale: Anchors directly on the article's tracked identifier CVE-2026-54051 and the fixed version 5.9.1, giving an exact asset-exposure list to prioritise the behavioural UCs. Note: npm package coverage in TVM may be partial; pair with a node_modules/network-ai package.json inventory sweep where TVM lacks npm visibility.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities.Vulnerabilities
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Host already upgraded to network-ai >= 5.9.1 but TVM cache not yet refreshed
  • Vendored/test copy of an old network-ai in a non-running directory flagged as installed
  • Vulnerability scanner version-parsing mismatch on a transitive dependency
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the reported SoftwareVersion is genuinely < 5.9.1 and the package is actually loaded by a running orchestrator (cross-check node process telemetry), not a stale/vendored copy. 2) Contain: schedule/expedite upgrade to network-ai >= 5.9.1 and, as defense-in-depth, strip broad wildcard allowlist entries (node */npm */git *). 3) Hunt: for each exposed host, run UC1–UC3 over the same window to confirm no active exploitation preceded the patch.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where CveId == "CVE-2026-54051"
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, SoftwareVendor, SoftwareName, SoftwareVersion, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by DeviceName asc

| where CveId == "CVE-2026-54051"
| summarize arg_max(TimeGenerated, *) by DeviceName
| project TimeGenerated, DeviceName, SoftwareVendor, SoftwareName, SoftwareVersion, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel
| order by DeviceName asc

Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-54051")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-54051")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).

Vulnerable network-ai (<=5.7.1) npm package present in node_modules AIDelivery High
Asset-inventory hunt that surfaces hosts with the vulnerable Jovancoding/Network-AI package on disk (CVE-2026-48814 / CVE-2026-46701). The package files land under node_modules\network-ai; presence is the precondition for the unauthenticated MCP SSE exposure. Triage version against the 5.7.2 fixed release. Rationale: Pivots on the concrete install path 'node_modules\network-ai' named in the advisory (bin/mcp-server.ts, lib/mcp-transport-sse.ts live under it). Cache-path exclusions cut npm/pnpm store noise so only real project installs surface for remediation against the 5.7.2 fix. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/network-ai/CVE-2026-46701/ • https://radar.offseq.com/threat/cve-2026-48814-cwe-306-missing-authentication-for--a37c283f4afc7554 • https://www.squaredtech.co/mcp-security-flaw-npm-package-ships-with-no-auth-by-default
ATT&CKT1190 T1195.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer workstation with network-ai intentionally installed for legitimate multi-agent orchestration
  • CI/CD build agent pulling the package during npm/pnpm install for tests
  • Security researcher detonating the package in a sandbox
  • Already-remediated 5.7.2+ install (identical path, patched version — verify package.json version field)
  • Monorepo with nested node_modules copies inflating host count
Suppression filter
| where FolderPath !has @"_cacache" and FolderPath !has @"\.npm\" and FolderPath !has @"\pnpm-store\"
Response runbook
1) Triage: open package.json under the flagged node_modules\network-ai path and read the version; if >=5.7.2 it is patched — close. If <=5.7.1, confirm whether the host runs the SSE MCP server (see companion process UC). 2) Contain: have the owner upgrade to network-ai 5.7.2+, or if the server is running, set a non-empty NETWORK_AI_MCP_SECRET and rebind the listener to 127.0.0.1 until patched. 3) Hunt: pivot the host into the process-launch and inbound-POST UCs to determine whether the vulnerable SSE server was ever started or reached from a non-loopback source.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FolderPath has @"node_modules\network-ai"
| where FolderPath !has @"_cacache" and FolderPath !has @"\.npm\" and FolderPath !has @"\pnpm-store\"
| summarize FirstSeen=min(Timestamp), LastSeen=max(Timestamp), FilesWritten=dcount(FileName), SamplePath=any(FolderPath), SawPackageJson=maxif(1, FileName =~ "package.json") by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by FirstSeen desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetFilePath has @"node_modules\network-ai"
| where TargetFilePath !has @"_cacache" and TargetFilePath !has @"\pnpm-store\"
| summarize FirstSeen=min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen=max(TimeGenerated), FilesWritten=dcount(TargetFileName), SamplePath=any(TargetFilePath) by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName
| order by FirstSeen desc

description: Detects on-disk presence of the Jovancoding/Network-AI npm package (CVE-2026-48814, vuln <=5.7.1), whose MCP SSE server defaults to an empty authorizing secret.
references:
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-r78r-rwrf-rjwp
  - https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/network-ai/CVE-2026-46701/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/20
tags:

  - attack.t1195.001
logsource:
  category: file_event
  product: windows
detection:
  selection:
    TargetFilename|contains: '\node_modules\network-ai\'
  filter_cache:
    TargetFilename|contains:
      - '_cacache'
      - '\pnpm-store\'

level: medium

| NOT TargetFileName=/(_cacache|pnpm-store)/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, TargetFileName], function=count())

network-ai MCP SSE server launched in non-stdio (vulnerable) mode AIInstallation High
Detects process creation of the Network-AI MCP server (command line contains both 'network-ai' and 'mcp-server') without the --stdio flag, i.e. the SSE listener that ships with the empty-default-secret authorization bypass. Stdio-mode invocations are excluded because the advisory's only safe path requires --stdio. Rationale: Uses the advisory's exact invocation tokens — the 'network-ai' package and its 'mcp-server' bin — and excludes --stdio, the only mode the advisory deems non-vulnerable. Surfaces the listening SSE server before exploitation rather than a generic node process. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/network-ai/CVE-2026-46701/ • https://www.squaredtech.co/mcp-security-flaw-npm-package-ships-with-no-auth-by-default
ATT&CKT1059 T1133
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Operator running the server locally bound to 127.0.0.1 for legitimate single-user use
  • Developer testing network-ai during feature work
  • Automated integration-test harness spinning the server up and tearing it down
  • Patched 5.7.2+ host where the secret is now enforced (still launches the same process — confirm version)
  • Container entrypoint launching the SSE server with a properly set NETWORK_AI_MCP_SECRET
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the on-disk network-ai version (<=5.7.1 = exposed) and whether NETWORK_AI_MCP_SECRET is set in the service/unit/container env; an empty/unset secret means _isAuthorized() returns true for all callers. 2) Contain: stop the SSE listener or rebind it to loopback, set a strong NETWORK_AI_MCP_SECRET, and upgrade to 5.7.2+. 3) Hunt: pull DeviceNetworkEvents/web logs for this host and run the inbound-POST /mcp UC to see whether any non-loopback caller already reached config_set / agent_spawn / token_* tools.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has "network-ai" and ProcessCommandLine has "mcp-server"
| where ProcessCommandLine !has "--stdio"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent

| where CommandLine has "network-ai" and CommandLine has "mcp-server"
| where CommandLine !has "--stdio"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Unauthenticated non-loopback POST /mcp tool invocation against network-ai SSE server AIExploitation Medium
Active-exploitation detection for CVE-2026-48814: a non-loopback caller (curl/SSRF/0.0.0.0 bind) issues POST /mcp to the Network-AI SSE server and gets HTTP 200 with no Authorization header, invoking MCP tools (config_set, agent_spawn, blackboard_write, token_*) against the empty default secret. Web/proxy logs carry the path+method+auth-header signal; EDR network telemetry carries the non-loopback inbound exposure. Rationale: Targets the advisory's PoC exactly — POST /mcp, HTTP 200, no auth, non-loopback source — using the web-log auth-header emptiness (ImWebSession.HttpRequestHeaderAuthorization) as the unauthenticated signal, with EDR inbound-non-loopback as a corroborating exposure pivot when proxy logs are absent. Datadog left blank: no generic web-access-log source in this tenant's listed schema carries POST/path/auth-header. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/network-ai/CVE-2026-46701/ • https://radar.offseq.com/threat/cve-2026-48814-cwe-306-missing-authentication-for--a37c283f4afc7554 • https://www.squaredtech.co/mcp-security-flaw-npm-package-ships-with-no-auth-by-default
ATT&CKT1190 T1059
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal vulnerability scanner / ASM probing the /mcp endpoint
  • Authorized local MCP client legitimately sending no Authorization header because the secret was never configured (still the vuln — worth flagging)
  • Reverse-proxy or container health check polling /mcp
  • Container/NAT bridge rewriting a loopback request so it appears as a non-loopback SrcIpAddr
  • Load balancer or service mesh sidecar fronting the SSE server with stripped auth headers
Suppression filter
| where SrcIpAddr !in ("127.0.0.1", "::1")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the destination host runs network-ai <=5.7.1 SSE mode, then inspect the request URL/body for invoked tool names (config_set, agent_spawn, blackboard_write, token_revoke) and check the source IP against known scanners and internal ranges. 2) Contain: block ingress to the MCP port from non-loopback sources at the host/SG firewall, set a strong NETWORK_AI_MCP_SECRET, restart the service, and upgrade to 5.7.2+. 3) Hunt: enumerate every POST /mcp 200 from this source across all hosts, review token_* and config_set invocations for tampering or stolen credentials, and rotate any tokens the blackboard/agent tools could have exposed.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "network-ai" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "mcp-server"
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "--stdio"
| where ActionType == "InboundConnectionAccepted"
| where RemoteIPType != "Loopback" and RemoteIP !in ("127.0.0.1", "::1")
| summarize Connections=count(), FirstSeen=min(Timestamp), LastSeen=max(Timestamp), Ports=make_set(LocalPort, 10) by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemoteIPType, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by FirstSeen desc
ImWebSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where HttpMethod =~ "POST"
| where Url has "/mcp"
| where HttpStatusCode == 200
| where isempty(HttpRequestHeaderAuthorization)
| where SrcIpAddr !in ("127.0.0.1", "::1")
| project TimeGenerated, SrcIpAddr, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, HttpMethod, Url, HttpStatusCode, UserAgent=HttpRequestHeaderUserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-48814", "CVE-2026-46701")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-48814", "CVE-2026-46701")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

gemini-mcp-tool @file path-traversal / sensitive-file exfiltration via Gemini CLI AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts Gemini CLI invocations (driven by the gemini-mcp-tool MCP server, CVE-2026-0755) whose command line carries an @file reference that escapes the working directory or targets sensitive files (@/etc/passwd, @~/.ssh/id_rsa, @../../secret). Article-specific because it keys on the Gemini CLI @file parser abuse used to read/exfiltrate arbitrary local files through the LLM channel. Rationale: Keys on the article's exact @file PoC strings (@/etc/passwd, @~/.ssh/id_rsa, @../../secret) scoped to a 'gemini' command line — the CVE-2026-0755 exfiltration sink. Cross-checked against the GitLab Advisory DB and Snyk entries which confirm the @file parser as the read/exfil vector. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/gemini-mcp-tool/CVE-2026-0755/ • https://security.snyk.io/vuln/SNYK-JS-GEMINIMCPTOOL-15091895 • https://www.sentinelone.com/vulnerability-database/cve-2026-0755/
ATT&CKT1005 T1552.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer legitimately referencing a sibling project with @../other-repo/file.ts for cross-repo code analysis
  • Power user analyzing their own dotfiles, e.g. gemini -p 'review @~/.bashrc'
  • CI job pointing Gemini CLI at an absolute build-artifact path outside the checkout dir
  • Security researcher reproducing the CVE in an authorized test lab
  • Legitimate analysis of /var/log or /etc config files by an ops engineer using Gemini CLI
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)@\.\./[A-Za-z0-9._-]+/(src|lib|test|tests|docs)/")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full ProcessCommandLine and the parent (expect node.exe running the gemini-mcp-tool MCP server). Confirm the @file target is outside the project working directory and is sensitive (passwd, id_rsa, .env, cloud creds). Identify who/what supplied the prompt (MCP client, chat session, automated agent). 2) Contain: kill the MCP server process, upgrade gemini-mcp-tool to >=1.1.6, and rotate any credential file referenced (SSH keys, tokens) since the content was sent to the Gemini API. 3) Hunt: review DeviceNetworkEvents for outbound to the Gemini API from this host around the event, search other hosts for the same gemini @traversal pattern, and check the MCP client logs for the originating untrusted prompt.

| order by Timestamp desc

gemini-mcp-tool Windows cmd.exe metacharacter injection (node -> cmd.exe breakout) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the CVE-2026-0755 Windows command-injection vector: the gemini-mcp-tool MCP server (node.exe) spawns cmd.exe to run the Gemini CLI, and unquoted prompt metacharacters break out of the gemini argument into arbitrary OS commands. Article-specific because it pivots on the node->cmd.exe chain carrying 'gemini' plus shell metacharacters (&, |, >, <, ^, `). Rationale: Pivots on the article's exact Windows injection mechanism — node-spawned cmd.exe running the Gemini CLI with unquoted metacharacters (the 'broken shell:false double-quote wrapping' the 1.1.6 fix removed). The node->cmd.exe->gemini chain is rare and the metacharacter requirement makes it alert-grade; corroborated by Penligent's execAsync sink analysis. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-4h5r-5jm8-jxjm • https://www.penligent.ai/hackinglabs/deep-analysis-of-gemini-mcp-tool-command-injection-cve-2026-0755-when-an-mcp-toolchain-hands-user-input-to-the-shell/ • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/gemini-mcp-tool/CVE-2026-0755/
ATT&CKT1059.003 T1202
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate prompt that genuinely contains '&' or '|' inside quotes on a patched (>=1.1.6) host where quoting now contains it
  • Developer asking gemini about shell syntax, e.g. a prompt mentioning 'cmd & redirect'
  • Build wrapper that intentionally chains gemini with another command via cmd.exe (&&)
  • Local reproduction of the CVE in an authorized lab
  • A node-based task runner that pipes Gemini CLI output to a file with '>'
Suppression filter
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)gemini[^\"]*[&|><^`]"
Response runbook
1) Triage: read the cmd.exe ProcessCommandLine and isolate what follows the metacharacter after the gemini token — that is the injected command. Enumerate child processes of this cmd.exe (DeviceProcessEvents where InitiatingProcessId == this ProcessId) to see what actually executed (whoami, certutil, powershell, net). 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the node MCP server and any spawned children, and upgrade gemini-mcp-tool to >=1.1.6. 3) Hunt: review DeviceNetworkEvents from the host for download/exfil by the injected child, search for the same node->cmd->gemini+metacharacter pattern fleet-wide, and trace the originating untrusted prompt in the MCP client logs.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "node.exe"
| where FileName =~ "cmd.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "gemini"
| where ProcessCommandLine matches regex @"(?i)gemini.*[&|><^`]"
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName endswith "\\cmd.exe"
| where ParentProcessName endswith "\\node.exe"
| where CommandLine has "gemini"
| where CommandLine matches regex @"(?i)gemini.*[&|><^`]"
| where not(TargetUserName endswith "$")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, TargetUserName, NewProcessName, ParentProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Article-specific behavioural hunt — Close Encounters of the Human Kind
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["9f1f11a708d393e0a4109ae189bc64f1f3e312653dcf317a2bd406f18ffcc507", "c0ad494457dcd9e964378760fb6aca86a23622045bca851d8f3ab49ec33978fe", "9896a6fcb9bb5ac1ec5297b4a65be3f647589adf7c37b45f3f7466decd6a4a7f", "e60ab99da105ee27ee09ea64ed8eb46d8edc92ee37f039dbc3e2bb9f587a33ba", "2915b3f8b703eb744fc54c81f4a9c67f", "bf9672ec85283fdf002d83662f0b08b7", "38de5b216c33833af710e88f7f64fc98", "dbd8dbecaa80795c135137d69921fdba"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

GitHub Security Advisories
2026-06-18 2 use cases 1 technique 2 kill-chain phases detected

Crawl4AI: Unauthenticated RCE via Chromium launch-argument injection in browser_config.extra_args

ATT&CKT1190
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] Chromium subprocess-launcher switch + --no-zygote co-occurrence (Crawl4AI extra_args RCE) · [LLM] Headless Chromium spawning shell/interpreter child (Crawl4AI injected-command exec)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Chromium subprocess-launcher switch + --no-zygote co-occurrence (Crawl4AI extra_args RCE) AIExploitation High
Hunts a single Chromium/headless-shell process whose command line carries --no-zygote together with a subprocess-launcher override (--utility-cmd-prefix / --renderer-cmd-prefix / --gpu-launcher / --browser-subprocess-path). This tuple is the execution signature of GHSA-r253-r9jw-qg44, where attacker-supplied browser_config.extra_args forces Chromium to fork/exec an arbitrary command. The combination is near-pathognomonic — legitimate Crawl4AI never sets a launcher-override prefix. Rationale: Uses the exact launcher-override switches named in the advisory (--utility-cmd-prefix/--renderer-cmd-prefix/--gpu-launcher/--browser-subprocess-path) gated on --no-zygote; per the Chromium zygote docs this combo is what forces a fresh fork/exec of the injected command, making it high-fidelity. Chromium switches are always lowercase canonical so no Datadog casing variants are needed. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/crawl4ai/GHSA-r253-r9jw-qg44/ • https://github.com/chromium/chromium/blob/main/docs/linux/zygote.md
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer debugging Chromium under valgrind/gdb using --renderer-cmd-prefix on a workstation
  • CEF/Electron app that sets --no-zygote for stability but never sets a launcher prefix (won't match — requires both)
  • Automated browser-performance testing harness intentionally wrapping subprocesses
  • Crash-reproduction tooling that prefixes the GPU process launcher
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("valgrind","gdb","--enable-logging=stderr") and InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("bash","sh","make","ctest"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full ProcessCommandLine and read the value after the launcher switch — that string IS the attacker command; capture the parent (Crawl4AI/uvicorn/python) and the runtime user. 2) Contain: kill the Chromium process tree, isolate/stop the Crawl4AI container, and rotate any secrets/tokens mounted into it (env, mounted files) since RCE ran as the runtime user. 3) Hunt: confirm the package is <=0.8.9, search for child processes the browser spawned (UC2), and review WAF/proxy logs for the POST /crawl* that delivered the payload (UC3).
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ProcessCommandLine contains "--no-zygote"
| where ProcessCommandLine contains "--utility-cmd-prefix"
    or ProcessCommandLine contains "--renderer-cmd-prefix"
    or ProcessCommandLine contains "--gpu-launcher"
    or ProcessCommandLine contains "--browser-subprocess-path"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActingProcessCommandLine contains "--no-zygote"
| where ActingProcessCommandLine contains "--utility-cmd-prefix"
    or ActingProcessCommandLine contains "--renderer-cmd-prefix"
    or ActingProcessCommandLine contains "--gpu-launcher"
    or ActingProcessCommandLine contains "--browser-subprocess-path"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName, ParentProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

description: Detects a Chromium/headless process launched with --no-zygote together with a subprocess-launcher override switch, the execution signature of GHSA-r253-r9jw-qg44 argument-injection RCE in Crawl4AI.

Headless Chromium spawning shell/interpreter child (Crawl4AI injected-command exec) AIExploitation High
Hunts a browser process (chrome/chromium/headless_shell) that forks a shell, network tool or scripting interpreter as a direct child. In normal operation Chromium only spawns more Chromium/crashpad processes; a /bin/sh, curl, python or nc child is the post-exploitation result of the extra_args launcher-prefix injection executing the attacker command as the container runtime user. Rationale: Pivots on the parent→child relationship the advisory describes ('causing Chromium to fork/exec an attacker-controlled command'); in a Crawl4AI container the browser legitimately only spawns more browser/crashpad processes, so a shell/curl/python child is high-fidelity. Datadog left empty because the standard linux.auditd schema exposes no parent-process field to anchor the chain. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/crawl4ai/GHSA-r253-r9jw-qg44/
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1203
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Custom crawler post-processing that legitimately pipes browser output through python/node
  • Container healthcheck scripts mis-parented under the browser during teardown
  • Debug builds invoking curl/wget from a browser test harness
  • Sandbox-disabled CI runs where the browser wrapper is a shell script
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("healthcheck","/usr/local/bin/entrypoint") )
Response runbook
1) Triage: read the child ProcessCommandLine and any network destination it contacted; identify the runtime user and whether the child reached out-of-band (curl/wget/nc to an external IP). 2) Contain: terminate the child + Chromium tree, stop/quarantine the container, and block the egress destination at the firewall; rotate mounted secrets and tokens. 3) Hunt: correlate to UC1 (launcher-switch cmdline) on the same host/time, review FileWritten events by the child for dropped payloads, and check DeviceNetworkEvents for follow-on C2 from the runtime user.

Crawl4AI path-traversal: crawler Python process writes to RCE-enabling paths (cron.d, authorized_keys, shell rc, site-packages) AIInstallation High
Hunts the exploitation primitive of GHSA-2jq4-q6vv-4cp3: the crawl4ai download sink (HTTP Content-Disposition filename or Playwright suggested_filename) escaping the downloads directory and writing attacker-controlled bytes to one of the four RCE sinks the advisory names. A web-crawler Python process should never write to /etc/cron.d, ~/.ssh/authorized_keys, shell rc files, or the Python import path, making this high-fidelity for the file-write-to-RCE escalation. Rationale: Uses the four RCE sinks the advisory explicitly names (/etc/cron.d/evil, ~/.ssh/authorized_keys, shell rc-file, Python module on import path) keyed to a python initiating process, the only attacker-influenced behaviour this file-write-to-RCE bug produces. Cross-checked the GHSA, GitLab GLAD (CWE-22+CWE-59, fix 0.9.0) and VulnCheck's related crawl4ai Docker advisory. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-2jq4-q6vv-4cp3 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/crawl4ai/GHSA-2jq4-q6vv-4cp3/ • https://www.vulncheck.com/advisories/crawl4ai-docker-api-local-file-inclusion-via-file-url-handling
ATT&CKT1190 T1053.003 T1098.004 T1546.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • pip/pip3 or poetry installing or upgrading a package writes legitimately into site-packages/dist-packages during a deploy of the crawler container
  • A maintenance/config-management Python script (Ansible/SaltStack module run via python) legitimately writing a cron job under /etc/cron.d
  • Developer running `python -m venv` or editable installs touching site-packages on a build host
  • Legitimate crawl4ai self-update writing its own package files
  • An ops automation Python tool rotating a service account's ~/.ssh/authorized_keys
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("pip","pip3","poetry","uv") or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("pip install","-m pip","setup.py","python -m venv")) and FolderPath has_any ("/site-packages/","/dist-packages/")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the InitiatingProcessCommandLine and FolderPath/FileName — confirm the writer is the crawl4ai service (uvicorn/gunicorn/python crawler) and not pip/deploy tooling, and inspect the written file's content (cron line, SSH key, rc payload). Check the SHA256 and the source URL/Content-Disposition that triggered the download. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the crawler process, remove the dropped file, and rotate any exposed SSH keys/service creds; confirm crawl4ai is upgraded to >=0.9.0 and CRAWL4AI_API_TOKEN auth is enabled. 3) Hunt: enumerate all files written by that python PID outside the downloads dir, review web/proxy logs for unauthenticated POSTs to the Docker /crawl endpoint with HTTPCrawlerConfig, and run UC2 to confirm whether cron/sshd executed the dropped payload.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName has "python" or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "crawl4ai"
| where (FolderPath has_any ("/etc/cron.d","/etc/cron.daily","/etc/cron.hourly","/var/spool/cron","/site-packages/","/dist-packages/"))
    or FileName =~ "authorized_keys"
    or FileName in~ (".bashrc",".bash_profile",".profile",".zshrc",".bash_login")
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc

Crawl4AI RCE confirmation: cron/sshd/shell executes payload dropped by crawler within correlation window AIActions on Objectives Medium
Second-stage confirmation for GHSA-2jq4-q6vv-4cp3: after the crawl4ai Python process writes to /etc/cron.d or ~/.ssh/authorized_keys, this correlates a subsequent process spawned by cron/crond/sshd (or a shell sourcing a modified rc) on the same host within 24h — evidence the arbitrary write actually escalated to code execution. Distinct pivot from UC1: process-execution telemetry joined to the file-write event, not the write alone. Rationale: Chains the UC1 drop (python writing /etc/cron.d or authorized_keys) to a cron/sshd-spawned execution on the same host inside 24h, confirming the advisory's stated escalation path (cron entry / authorized_keys -> RCE) actually fired rather than just the write attempt. ATT&CKT1053.003 T1098.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate ops automation that drops a cron job via a python config tool and the new cron job fires on schedule within the window
  • An admin adding their SSH key to authorized_keys via a python helper, then logging in via SSH (sshd-spawned shell)
  • Normal scheduled cron tasks running on a host where a python deploy touched /etc/cron.d for unrelated reasons
  • CI/CD agent (python-based) provisioning cron and SSH access during a build, followed by sshd sessions
Suppression filter
| where AccountName !endswith "$" and not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("/usr/lib/snapd","unattended-upgrade","apt.systemd"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: open the joined record — confirm the cron/sshd child process maps to the file the crawler wrote (cron line content or the SSH session's source IP and key). Compare ExecFile/ProcessCommandLine against the dropped payload. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the spawned process tree, disable the malicious cron entry and remove the unauthorized authorized_keys line, and terminate any active SSH sessions from unexpected source IPs. 3) Hunt: pivot on AccountName and source IP across DeviceLogonEvents for further SSH access, review outbound DeviceNetworkEvents from the spawned process for C2, and confirm crawl4ai is upgraded to >=0.9.0 across the fleet.

    | project DropTime = Timestamp, DeviceId, DeviceName, DropPath = FolderPath, DropFile = FileName;

| order by ExecTime desc

service-brother identity reaching admin/protected or mutating endpoints (post-bypass abuse) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Surfaces requests asserting auth-user: service-brother that target sensitive routes (/admin, /protected, /graphql, /internal) or use mutating methods (POST/PUT/DELETE/PATCH) — i.e. the validateToken() bypass actually being used to act on protected resources rather than merely probed. Baseline legitimate service-brother destinations/methods and treat deviations as exploitation. Rationale: Combines the article's bypass marker (service-brother) with sensitive routes named in the fix (consumers: rest, graphql) and the example endpoint (/protected); mutating methods + 2xx on protected paths is the 'bypass was used' signal (reviewer #2/#4). The Splunk Web-datamodel half is the access-pattern net since CIM Web does not model request headers — confirm the header in WAF logs. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/@acastellon/auth/GHSA-gfj5-979r-92pw/ • https://cvereports.com/reports/GHSA-GFJ5-979R-92PW
ATT&CKT1190 T1078 T1556
Data sourcesWeb.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate post-fix mTLS service-brother traffic that still carries the header while writing to admin/internal APIs
  • Internal automation/cron service accounts performing scheduled POST/PUT to /internal or /config
  • GraphQL introspection or schema-sync jobs hitting /graphql
  • Monitoring/synthetic probes that exercise /protected
  • Blue-team validation traffic after deploying the patch
Suppression filter
| where not(Message has_any ("client-cert","mtls","x-client-verify: SUCCESS")) and not(ipv4_is_private(SourceIP))
Response runbook
1) Triage: for each hit, confirm the request both asserts service-brother AND came from outside the service mesh; check the HTTP status — a 2xx on an admin/mutating route means the bypass succeeded. Diff the destination/method against the service-brother baseline. 2) Contain: revoke/rotate any secrets or data the route could expose, block the source, and enforce mTLS (TRUSTED_MTLS_SERVICES allowlist) so header-only identity is rejected. 3) Hunt: reconstruct the full session from that source across all routes, look for data exfil or further writes, and check downstream services that trusted the forwarded identity.

| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, DestinationHostName, RequestMethod, RequestURL, DeviceProduct, Message
| sort by TimeGenerated desc

Vulnerable @acastellon/auth (<2.3.0) package present on host AIExploitation Medium
Inventory hunt for the affected npm package on disk — node_modules\@acastellon\auth\auth.js / package.json — which contains the spoofable validateToken() bypass below v2.3.0. Identifies servers running the vulnerable dependency so they can be patched before the header bypass is exploited; analyst confirms the exact version (2.2.0 vulnerable, fixed in 2.3.0+) from the package.json. Rationale: Targets the article's exact affected package and source file (auth.js) at the canonical npm scoped path; presence below v2.3.0 is the vulnerable surface for the bypass. This is the one detection expressible on endpoint/file telemetry since the header bypass itself is invisible to EDR. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-gfj5-979r-92pw • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/@acastellon/auth/GHSA-gfj5-979r-92pw/ • https://cvereports.com/reports/GHSA-GFJ5-979R-92PW
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer laptops or CI/build agents that pull the package into a test/scratch project
  • Hosts already upgraded to 2.3.0+ — identical file paths, only the package.json version differs (must be checked)
  • Security researcher reproducing the advisory in a lab
  • A transitive dependency vendoring the package that is not actually exposed on a network route
  • Container image build layers scanned at rest
Response runbook
1) Triage: on each hit, read node_modules/@acastellon/auth/package.json and confirm "version" < 2.3.0 (2.2.0 is the named vulnerable build); determine whether the host serves a public-facing route using validateToken(). 2) Contain: for internet-facing vulnerable hosts, deploy the edge WAF header-strip rule (auth-user, is-*) as an interim mitigation and prioritize upgrade to >=2.3.0 with mTLS service auth. 3) Hunt: cross-reference these hosts against the WAF header-bypass UCs to see if any received auth-user: service-brother traffic, and sweep the SBOM/lockfiles fleet-wide for the same dependency.

netlicensing-mcp get_product called with path-traversal product_number (../token) AIExploitation High
Hunts MCP/application-log evidence of the netlicensing_get_product tool being invoked with a product_number containing path-traversal sequences (../, %2e%2e, %2f, %5c). This is the raw attacker input for GHSA-hxpf-9xvq-wph8, captured before httpx normalizes /product/../token into /token. Rationale: Uses the exact MCP tool name (netlicensing_get_product) plus the article's documented traversal payloads (../token and the encoded variants %2e%2e/%2f/%5c the remediation adds regression tests for). A Product-fetch tool receiving path separators in its identifier argument has no legitimate use. Defender/Falcon/CloudWatch empty: the payload is an in-process MCP tool argument, not a Windows process command line or AWS API call. NetLicensing REST request URL containing both /product/ and traversal-to-token (wire signature) AIExploitation High
Hunts forward-proxy / SWG logs for a single request to go.netlicensing.io (or backup netlicensing.labs64.com) whose URL contains the /product/ segment together with /token or an encoded traversal sequence — the on-the-wire artefact when the percent-encoded GHSA-hxpf-9xvq-wph8 payload survives httpx normalization. Legitimate traffic hits /product OR /token, never both in one URL. Rationale: Anchors on the confirmed REST base host go.netlicensing.io (+backup netlicensing.labs64.com) and the structural impossibility of a legitimate URL containing both /product/ and a token/traversal segment. Targets the encoded variants (%2e%2e/%2f/%5c) that survive WHATWG normalization to the wire. Datadog empty: no web-proxy source in this tenant's source list; Defender/Falcon empty: no HTTP path visibility inside HTTPS to a SaaS endpoint. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/netlicensing-mcp/GHSA-hxpf-9xvq-wph8/ • https://netlicensing.io/wiki/restful-api
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesWeb.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment

  • Authorized pentest validating the GHSA-hxpf-9xvq-wph8 fix
  • A proxy that pre-normalizes URLs and rewrites %2e%2e (would suppress, not false-positive)
  • Manual cURL testing by an engineer reproducing the advisory
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Confirm the proxy log shows a single request to go.netlicensing.io with /product/ + token/traversal in the URL; identify the SourceIP (the MCP server host) and any authenticated SourceUserName. 2) CONTAIN: Block/inspect the source if unauthorized; rotate the NetLicensing API key the MCP server uses and any potentially-exposed APIKEY tokens; ensure the package is upgraded to >=0.1.8. 3) HUNT: Pivot to MCP/app logs (UC1) for the originating get_product call and to UC4 for bare /token hits from the same host that the literal ../ form would have produced.

| order by TimeGenerated desc

netlicensing-mcp get_product response leaking APIKEY/token fields (redaction bypass) AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts MCP/application logs for a netlicensing_get_product (Product-typed) tool call whose output contains token-specific fields — tokenType APIKEY, ROLE_APIKEY_ADMIN, or a console_url under /#/tokens/. Per GHSA-hxpf-9xvq-wph8 these are exactly the fields redact_token_read() would mask; a Product tool returning them is direct evidence the traversal reached /token and leaked a plaintext key. Rationale: Pairs the Product tool name with the exact token-leak markers from the advisory's PoC output (tokenType=APIKEY, role ROLE_APIKEY_ADMIN, console_url https://ui.netlicensing.io/#/tokens/<key>). The legitimate token tools (get_token/list_tokens) may emit these too, but a PRODUCT tool emitting them is unambiguous bypass evidence. Hunting tier because it depends on the deployment logging tool responses. Defender/Falcon/CloudWatch empty: app-layer response content unavailable to those platforms. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/netlicensing-mcp/GHSA-hxpf-9xvq-wph8/ • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-hxpf-9xvq-wph8
ATT&CKT1212 T1552
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Verbose debug logging that echoes a legitimate get_token response near a get_product log line (correlation artefact)
  • Replay of the published PoC output in a test/log-sample dataset
  • A custom downstream tool that legitimately enriches product data with token metadata (rare; should be validated)
  • Log aggregation that interleaves get_product and get_token messages making string co-occurrence appear in one record
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Confirm the record is a get_product RESPONSE (not a get_token call) carrying a real (unmasked) APIKEY number/console_url. Note the MCP client identity and the leaked token's role. 2) CONTAIN: Immediately revoke/rotate the disclosed APIKEY token — if ROLE_APIKEY_ADMIN, treat the entire NetLicensing account as compromised (full read/write/delete) and rotate the account owner credential; upgrade to >=0.1.8. 3) HUNT: Enumerate all get_product calls from this client (UC1) to scope which keys were exfiltrated, and review NetLicensing audit/console activity for use of the leaked admin token.
Syslog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where SyslogMessage has "netlicensing_get_product"
| where SyslogMessage has "ROLE_APIKEY_ADMIN" or (SyslogMessage has "tokenType" and SyslogMessage has "APIKEY") or SyslogMessage contains "/#/tokens/"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, ProcessName, HostName, SyslogMessage
| order by TimeGenerated desc
source:linux.syslog netlicensing_get_product AND ("ROLE_APIKEY_ADMIN" OR "tokenType" OR "/#/tokens/")
APPLICATION
index=* "netlicensing_get_product" ("ROLE_APIKEY_ADMIN" OR "tokenType" OR "/#/tokens/")
| table _time host source _raw
| sort - _time
First-seen access to NetLicensing /token REST endpoint from MCP egress source AIActions on Objectives Medium
Behavioral baseline over proxy logs: flags a source host issuing GET /core/v2/rest/token to go.netlicensing.io when that source has no prior history of token-endpoint access in the baseline window. This covers the GHSA-hxpf-9xvq-wph8 case where the literal ../token payload is normalized by httpx to a bare /token request that carries no traversal artefact on the wire. Rationale: Catches the literal ../token variant that UC2 misses (httpx collapses /product/../token to a bare /token GET). Because the dedicated token tools also legitimately hit /token, this relies on a 14-day per-source baseline so only newly-token-active sources surface — Medium confidence by design. Anchored on the confirmed REST path /core/v2/rest/token. Datadog empty (no proxy source in tenant list); Defender/Falcon empty (no HTTPS path visibility). Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/netlicensing-mcp/GHSA-hxpf-9xvq-wph8/ • https://netlicensing.io/wiki/restful-api

Data sourcesWeb.All_Traffic

Response runbook

If the source is the MCP server host and no legitimate token tool was invoked, treat as the normalized traversal. 2) CONTAIN: Rotate any APIKEY tokens the source could have read; confirm package >=0.1.8. 3) HUNT: Add confirmed-benign token-using sources to an allowlist, then widen the window to look for repeated first-seen token access across other hosts.

    | summarize by SourceIP;

Vulnerable googleapis/mcp-toolbox (< 1.4.0) — CVE-2026-11718 opaque-token auth bypass present AIExploitation Medium
Surfaces any managed host whose vulnerability feed reports CVE-2026-11717 / CVE-2026-11718 against the googleapis/mcp-toolbox Go package. This hunts the specific auth-bypass advisory (validateOpaqueToken accepting introspection responses that omit the mandatory RFC 7662 "active" field), not a generic missing-patch query — it anchors on the exact CVE IDs and the mcp-toolbox software name. Rationale: Anchors on the article's two concrete identifiers (CVE-2026-11717/11718) and the affected package name github.com/googleapis/mcp-toolbox; deterministic CVE-feed match, so FP is limited to stale-inventory rather than behavioural noise. Cross-checked the GHSA against CyberPress, CyberSecurityNews and the upstream GitHub issue #3076 which confirm fix version 1.4.0 and CVSS 4.0 9.3. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-wcpr-6g7x-p44r • https://github.com/googleapis/mcp-toolbox/issues/3076 • https://cyberpress.org/mcp-toolbox-vulnerability-exposed/ • https://cybersecuritynews.com/mcp-toolbox-vulnerability/ • https://cve.threatint.eu/CVE/CVE-2026-11718
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Host already upgraded to 1.4.0+ but the TVM/scanner snapshot is stale and a re-scan is still pending
  • CVE feed maps the Go module under a SoftwareName that doesn't contain 'toolbox' (vendor naming drift), so a real device is missed rather than false-positive
  • Isolated dev/CI box intentionally pinned to an old version inside a non-routable network
  • Vendored / build-artifact copy of the module flagged as 'installed' though no toolbox service is actually listening
  • Container image scanned by the feed though the image is ephemeral and already destroyed

Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: For each dest/DeviceName, confirm a mcp-toolbox process is actually listening (default port 5000) and note whether it's internet-facing; pull SoftwareVersion to verify it is genuinely < 1.4.0 and not a stale record. 2) CONTAIN: Patch to >= 1.4.0 (the fixed release); if patching is delayed, restrict the toolbox's network exposure to trusted clients and verify the configured OAuth introspection endpoint is a trusted, RFC 7662-compliant server that always returns the 'active' field. 3) HUNT: Review toolbox/auth access logs for tool invocations that succeeded against an introspection response lacking 'active', and check for the sibling advisories on the same hosts (CVE-2026-9739 SSE DNS-rebinding, and the issuer-field bypass variant).

Internet-facing host running vulnerable mcp-toolbox (< 1.4.0) — exposed auth-bypass surface AIExploitation Medium
Prioritises CVE-2026-11718 exposure by cross-referencing the mcp-toolbox software inventory (version < 1.4.0, the fixed release) against assets flagged internet-facing. Targets the externally reachable instances where an attacker-influenced or non-compliant introspection endpoint could trigger the validateOpaqueToken bypass — a different pivot (inventory + exposure join) than the raw CVE-feed match. Rationale: Uses the article's fixed-version boundary (1.4.0) for an explicit parse_version comparison and the package name, then joins DeviceInfo.IsInternetFacing to rank externally exposed instances — the highest-risk targets for a remote token-validation bypass (T1190). Sentinel lacks the DeviceTvmSoftwareInventory table, so the Sentinel variant expresses the same intent via the CVE feed joined to internet-facing assets. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-wcpr-6g7x-p44r • https://cyberpress.org/mcp-toolbox-vulnerability-exposed/ • https://github.com/googleapis/mcp-toolbox/issues/3076
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • SoftwareVersion carries a pre-release/build suffix (e.g. '1.4.0-rc1') that passes the regex but is functionally patched
  • IsInternetFacing flag is stale — host has since been moved behind a firewall or load balancer
  • mcp-toolbox present only as a transitive SDK/library, not the exposed listening server
  • Reverse proxy / WAF fronts the toolbox so the flagged host is exposed but the toolbox port is not actually reachable
  • Inventory shows old version string cached after an in-place binary swap to 1.4.0
Suppression filter
| where SoftwareVersion !startswith "1.4." and SoftwareVersion !startswith "v1.4."
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Confirm the toolbox listener is reachable from the public IP (probe the configured port, default 5000) and that SoftwareVersion is genuinely < 1.4.0; validate the OAuth introspection endpoint it trusts is RFC 7662-compliant and always emits 'active'. 2) CONTAIN: Take the external exposure offline or front it with auth-enforcing proxy; upgrade to >= 1.4.0. 3) HUNT: Pull the toolbox/auth logs for the exposure window and look for tool-invocation successes tied to introspection responses missing 'active', plus the related SSE DNS-rebinding (CVE-2026-9739) and issuer-omission bypass on the same internet-facing fleet.

Kirby Panel install surface accessed on a live site (CVE-2026-54003) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts any HTTP request to the Kirby Panel installation routes (/panel/installation or /api/system/install) on a production site. An already-initialised Kirby install never legitimately serves these routes, so traffic here on a live site indicates external-initialization probing or exploitation of CVE-2026-54003 via the spoofable Forwarded/X-Client-IP/X-Real-IP headers. Rationale: Uses the exact Kirby install routes /panel/installation and /api/system/install named in the advisory and confirmed in Kirby docs; on a live site any non-404/403 hit is anomalous. Client-IP filtering is deliberately omitted because behind a reverse proxy the logged cIP is the proxy, not the attacker (the spoofed IP lives in the Forwarded/X-Real-IP header). Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-whxw-24jc-cwmv • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/getkirby/cms/CVE-2026-54003/ • https://github.com/getkirby/kirby/releases/tag/5.4.4
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internet background scanners probing common CMS/admin paths like /panel
  • Legitimate first-time deployment of a brand-new Kirby site within the maintenance window
  • Authorized vulnerability scanning or pentest engagement against the site
  • Synthetic uptime/availability monitors crawling Panel routes
  • Staging/dev Kirby instance shipping access logs into the same workspace
Suppression filter
| where toint(scStatus) !in (404, 403)
Response runbook
1) Triage: Pull all install-route hits; check the response code and whether this host is a brand-new deploy (no admin yet) versus an established site. Cross-reference proxy logs for the Forwarded/X-Client-IP/X-Real-IP header and the true client IP. 2) Contain: If the site was already live, treat as compromise — set 'api'=>false or take Panel offline, and update Kirby to 4.9.4/5.4.4. 3) Hunt: Pivot to UC 'admin account created' to see if a POST succeeded, then review Panel logins, new users, and uploaded templates/plugins for webshells.
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where csUriStem has "/panel/installation" or csUriStem has "/api/system/install"
| where toint(scStatus) !in (404, 403)
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, sSiteName, cIP, csMethod, csUriStem, csUriQuery, scStatus, csUserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Kirby remote first-admin creation via unauthenticated install POST (CVE-2026-54003) AIInstallation High
Alerts on a successful POST to the Kirby install API (/api/system/install or /panel/installation) returning a 2xx status. On a publicly reachable Kirby site this means the first admin account was created without authentication by abusing the Forwarded/X-Client-IP/X-Real-IP isLocal bypass — the actual exploitation and persistence step of CVE-2026-54003. Rationale: Keys on the exact install API route plus method=POST and a 2xx response — a combination that on an already-deployed Kirby site means an admin was actually created without auth, which is the precise impact the advisory describes. Status filter separates successful exploitation from harmless scanner probes. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-whxw-24jc-cwmv • https://advisories.gitlab.com/composer/getkirby/cms/CVE-2026-54003/ • https://github.com/getkirby/kirby/releases/tag/5.4.4
ATT&CKT1190 T1136
Data sourcesWeb
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Intentional first-time install by an admin with panel.install enabled during a deploy window
  • DevOps automation that provisions the first admin via the install endpoint
  • Fresh staging/dev deploy that legitimately has no users yet
  • A redeploy that wiped the accounts/ directory and is being re-initialised on purpose
Response runbook
1) Triage: Confirm scStatus 2xx on the POST and verify the site was already live (had an admin) rather than a planned new deploy; capture cIP and the true client IP from proxy Forwarded/X-Real-IP headers. 2) Contain: Immediately delete the attacker-created admin account, invalidate all Panel sessions, set 'api'=>false or take the Panel offline, and upgrade to Kirby 4.9.4/5.4.4. 3) Hunt: Review all Panel logins and actions from the new account, content/blueprint/template changes, additional user creations, and any uploaded PHP for webshells; pivot on the source IP across other sites.
W3CIISLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where csUriStem has "/api/system/install" or csUriStem has "/panel/installation"
| where csMethod == "POST"
| where toint(scStatus) in (200, 201)
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, sSiteName, cIP, csMethod, csUriStem, csUriQuery, scStatus, csUserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Internet-facing PraisonAI platform booting on default JWT secret (CVE-2026-47410) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts hosts running praisonai-platform's documented entry point bound to 0.0.0.0 (python -m praisonai_platform / uvicorn praisonai_platform.api.app). Because PLATFORM_JWT_SECRET/PLATFORM_ENV are set nowhere in the package, any such exposed instance signs session JWTs with the public literal 'dev-secret-change-me' and is pre-auth forgeable. Surfaces the vulnerable deployment so an analyst can confirm the env vars are actually set. Rationale: Uses the advisory's exact documented entry point strings (praisonai_platform, uvicorn praisonai_platform.api.app, --host 0.0.0.0) — the article states neither PLATFORM_JWT_SECRET nor PLATFORM_ENV is set anywhere in the package, so any matching internet-bound process is the vulnerable default state. Cross-checked against GHSA-3qg8-5g3r-79v5 and the GitLab advisory. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-3qg8-5g3r-79v5 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/praisonai-platform/CVE-2026-47410/ • https://thehackernews.com/2026/05/praisonai-cve-2026-44338-auth-bypass.html
ATT&CKT1190 T1078
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer/staging instance where PLATFORM_JWT_SECRET is exported in the shell or systemd unit (not visible on cmdline) so the deployment is actually safe
  • Container CI smoke-test that boots the server transiently then tears down
  • Reverse-proxy fronted deployment where 0.0.0.0 binding is intentional and the secret is set via Docker secret/env-file
  • Security team running the package in a lab to reproduce the advisory
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: On the surfaced host, check the running process environment for PLATFORM_JWT_SECRET (e.g. `cat /proc/<pid>/environ | tr '\0' '\n' | grep PLATFORM_`) — if absent or equal to 'dev-secret-change-me', the instance is forgeable. 2) CONTAIN: Pull the service off the public interface (firewall the port / bind to localhost), export a strong random PLATFORM_JWT_SECRET and PLATFORM_ENV=production, restart, and invalidate all outstanding sessions (every issued JWT was signed with the public key). 3) HUNT: Review the web/proxy logs for this service for the workspace-member enumeration and owner-gated DELETE activity (see companion UCs) to determine if forgery was already exercised.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has "praisonai_platform"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("uvicorn", "-m praisonai_platform", "praisonai_platform.api.app")
| where ProcessCommandLine has "0.0.0.0"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "0.0.0.0"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

PraisonAI workspace/member destruction via owner-gated routes (forged-JWT takeover impact) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects successful DELETE requests against praisonai-platform's owner-gated routes — DELETE /workspaces/{workspace_id} (full workspace + contained resources) and DELETE /{workspace_id}/members/{user_id} (legitimate-member eviction). With the auth bypass these are reachable by an attacker holding only a forged owner JWT, yielding irrecoverable lock-out (A:H). Rationale: Pivots on the advisory's exact owner-gated route names (DELETE /workspaces/{workspace_id} and DELETE /{workspace_id}/members/{user_id}) called out as the resource-destruction / lock-out endpoints. Web-tier only because forgery and verification happen in-process with no endpoint signal. Cross-checked against the GitHub and GitLab advisories. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-3qg8-5g3r-79v5 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/praisonai-platform/CVE-2026-47410/
ATT&CKT1485 T1531 T1606
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Genuine workspace owner deleting a workspace they no longer need
  • Admin offboarding a departed employee via DELETE /{workspace_id}/members/{user_id}
  • Automated cleanup job pruning stale demo/trial workspaces
  • QA teardown of test workspaces in a non-prod tenant
Suppression filter
| where CIp !in ("<known-admin-egress-ips>")  // remove this line and substitute your real admin egress CIDRs via an ip range watchlist; alternatively join to an allowlist of legitimate owner UPNs on CsUsername
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Pull the source IP, User-Agent and any captured CsUsername for the DELETE; correlate the same source against the member-enumeration UC and the host-exposure UC — a delete preceded by a GET /{workspace_id}/members burst from the same IP strongly indicates forged-token takeover rather than a real owner. 2) CONTAIN: If forgery is suspected, rotate PLATFORM_JWT_SECRET immediately (this invalidates every forged and legitimate token), block the source IP at the proxy/WAF, and restore the deleted workspace/members from backup. 3) HUNT: Enumerate all DELETE and owner-gated mutation calls (member role changes, resource deletes) from the same IP/UA across every workspace to scope the blast radius.

     or CsUriStem matches regex @"(?i)/[^/]+/members/[^/]+/?$"
| where ScStatus between (200 .. 299)
| project TimeGenerated, CIp, CsUsername, CsHost, CsMethod, CsUriStem, ScStatus, UserAgent
| order by TimeGenerated desc

PraisonAI workspace member enumeration harvesting owner user_ids (pre-takeover recon) AIReconnaissance Medium
Detects a single source listing members across many workspaces via GET /{workspace_id}/members. The advisory states the response (List[MemberResponse], each carrying user_id and role) is how an attacker learns a workspace owner's uuid4 id — the one extra fact needed to forge an owner token and pivot from anonymous bypass to targeted owner takeover. Rationale: Pivots on the advisory's named recon primitive: GET /{workspace_id}/members returns each member's user_id and role to any member, which is exactly how an attacker obtains an owner's otherwise-unguessable uuid4 id. Fan-out across many workspaces from one source is the high-fidelity discriminator versus a real owner viewing their own one workspace. Threshold-based so no Sigma. Cross-checked against GHSA-3qg8-5g3r-79v5. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-3qg8-5g3r-79v5 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/praisonai-platform/CVE-2026-47410/
ATT&CKT1087 T1606
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Admin/support console legitimately iterating member lists across many workspaces
  • Billing or analytics service account that periodically reads membership for all workspaces
  • Internal vulnerability scanner crawling the API surface
  • A power user who genuinely owns/belongs to many workspaces and opens each members page in a session
Suppression filter
| where UserAgent !has "PraisonAI-Admin" and CsUsername !in ("svc-billing", "svc-analytics")  // suppress known internal service accounts/tools that legitimately sweep member lists
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Identify the source IP/UA and whether it maps to a known service account or admin tool; an unfamiliar IP reading 5+ workspaces' member lists in minutes is the harvesting signal. 2) CONTAIN: Block the source at the proxy/WAF and, if the host-exposure UC confirms the instance runs on the default secret, rotate PLATFORM_JWT_SECRET and set PLATFORM_ENV=production to kill the forgery primitive before owner-id harvesting can be weaponised. 3) HUNT: Pivot the same source forward in time to any owner-gated mutation (DELETE workspace/member, role change) — see the destruction UC — to confirm whether harvested ids were used to forge owner tokens.

PraisonAI multiedit arbitrary write to SSH/shell/cloud-cred files by python process AIInstallation High
Hunts a PraisonAI agent (python) abusing the unvalidated `multiedit` filepath parameter to overwrite security-sensitive files outside the workspace — ~/.ssh/authorized_keys, ~/.bashrc/.profile, ~/.aws/credentials, .env. This is the write primitive of GHSA-29w3-p9w9-wc47 used for persistence/privilege escalation, and is article-specific because it ties the named tool's host process to writes against the exact protected paths the advisory calls out. Rationale: Uses the advisory's exact write-target paths (~/.ssh/authorized_keys, ~/.bashrc, ~/.aws/credentials, .env) bound to the python host process of the named multiedit tool; high-fidelity because authorized_keys/.bashrc writes by an interpreter are rare. Cross-checked against the GitHub Advisory and the broader PraisonAI path-traversal advisory cluster. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-29w3-p9w9-wc47 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pkg/pypi/praisonai/ • https://www.csoonline.com/article/4171215/praisonai-vulnerability-gets-scanned-within-4-hours-of-disclosure.html
ATT&CKT1098.004 T1546.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate Python application/deployment script writing its own project-local .env file inside the app workspace
  • Configuration-management tooling written in Python (Ansible local modules, custom provisioning) updating ~/.bashrc or ~/.ssh/authorized_keys
  • Developer Jupyter/python session legitimately editing dotfiles during environment setup
  • Cloud SDK helper writing ~/.aws/credentials during an authorized `aws configure`-style automation
Suppression filter
| where not (FileName =~ ".env" and FolderPath has_any ("/site-packages/","/node_modules/","/.venv/","/venv/"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the full InitiatingProcessCommandLine and parent chain — confirm the writer is a PraisonAI agent/CLI (look for 'praisonai'/'multiedit') and whether the target path is outside the agent's intended workspace. Capture the before/after file contents (esp. authorized_keys new keys, .bashrc appended commands). 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the agent process, revoke/rotate any keys or cloud credentials in the touched files, and remove attacker-added authorized_keys entries. 3) Hunt: pin praisonai version (must be >= 4.6.61); search the same host and peer agents for multiedit reads of secrets (see credential-read UC), unexpected outbound C2 from python, and downstream use of stolen ~/.aws/credentials in cloud logs.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName has "python" or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("praisonai","multiedit")
| where (FileName =~ "authorized_keys" and FolderPath has "/.ssh/")
     or FileName in~ (".bashrc",".bash_profile",".profile",".zshrc",".bash_aliases")
     or (FileName =~ "credentials" and FolderPath has "/.aws/")
     or FileName =~ ".env"
     or (FileName in~ ("id_rsa","id_ed25519","authorized_keys") and FolderPath has "/.ssh/")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FolderPath, FileName, ActionType, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
