PraisonAI multiedit secret-file read (/etc/shadow, id_rsa, .aws/credentials, .env) by python AIActions on Objectives Medium
Hunts the read/exfil primitive of GHSA-29w3-p9w9-wc47: a python-hosted PraisonAI agent opening sensitive secret files via the unvalidated multiedit filepath (incl. dry_run diff leakage). Targets the exact files the advisory names — /etc/shadow, ~/.ssh/id_rsa, ~/.aws/credentials, .env. Requires open/read telemetry (auditd watches or CrowdStrike FileOpenInfo) since Defender DeviceFileEvents does not emit read-only access. Rationale: Targets the precise secret paths the advisory cites as readable via the unvalidated multiedit tool, scoped to the python interpreter that hosts PraisonAI; defender_kql intentionally left empty because Defender DeviceFileEvents does not record read-only opens, so the detection leans on auditd/FileOpenInfo. Validated against the GHSA write-up and the PraisonAI advisory cluster. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-29w3-p9w9-wc47 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pkg/pypi/praisonai/
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1552.004 T1003.008
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate Python automation reading its own project .env at startup (inside workspace)
  • Python-based config tooling reading ~/.aws/credentials during an authorized cloud operation
  • Backup/inventory agents written in Python touching /etc/shadow on a hardened host
  • Security scanners or compliance scripts (python) auditing shadow/SSH key permissions
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the reader is PraisonAI (command line / parent contains praisonai|multiedit) and that the file is outside any legitimate workspace; check whether dry_run diff output of these files was returned to a chat/agent transcript. 2) Contain: isolate the host, stop the agent, and immediately rotate exposed secrets — SSH private keys, AWS access keys, anything in the read .env; force-expire AWS sessions. 3) Hunt: correlate the read timestamp with outbound python network connections (possible exfil) and with subsequent use of the leaked AWS keys in CloudTrail from new source IPs; check peer hosts running the same vulnerable PraisonAI build.

Vulnerable PraisonAI install/upgrade activity (verify version >= 4.6.61) AIWeaponization Low
Surfaces hosts where the vulnerable PraisonAI package (pip:praisonai < 4.6.61, fixed for GHSA-29w3-p9w9-wc47) is being installed or upgraded, so exposure can be confirmed and patched. Exposure/hardening hunt rather than active-attack detection; pair with endpoint software inventory to confirm the resolved version. Rationale: Uses the advisory's concrete fixed-version boundary (praisonai 4.6.61) and package name to flag install/upgrade events for exposure triage; confidence is Low because it catches install commands (not container-baked images) and does not by itself prove the resolved version — confirm via software inventory/TVM. Cross-referenced with the GitHub Advisory fixed-version metadata. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-29w3-p9w9-wc47 • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pkg/pypi/praisonai/
ATT&CKT1190 T1059.006
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Patching activity that installs praisonai >= 4.6.61 (the safe version) — benign, the goal state
  • CI/CD build agents reinstalling dependencies including praisonai during normal pipeline runs
  • Developer creating a fresh virtualenv and pip-installing the project's requirements
  • Container image bake steps shown as process events on build hosts
Response runbook
1) Triage: from the command line determine the requested version; then query endpoint software inventory / `pip show praisonai` on the host to get the actually-installed version and compare to 4.6.61. 2) Contain: if < 4.6.61, prioritize upgrade to >= 4.6.61; on internet-exposed or chatbot deployments using auto_approve_tools=True, restrict the multiedit tool or gate it behind approval until patched. 3) Hunt: enumerate all hosts/containers running praisonai (inventory + this query org-wide), then run the multiedit read/write UCs against those hosts to check for actual exploitation before the patch landed.

Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).

Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] PraisonAI MCP server (node) spawns shell/LOLBin child after external connection · OAuth consent / suspicious app grant
Node.js MCP server reachable from public clients (unauthenticated MCPServer exposure) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the core PraisonAI MCPServer flaw: startHttp() calls listen(port) with no host arg, binding to the unspecified address (observed '::') so the HTTP JSON-RPC transport is reachable on all interfaces. This surfaces Node listeners accepting inbound connections from public source IPs — the precondition for unauthenticated tools/call. Rationale: Targets the article's root cause — httpServer.listen(port) with no host binds to '::' (the PoV's observed bindAddress), making the MCP HTTP transport reachable off-host. Inbound-accept on node from a Public RemoteIP is the exposure that unauthenticated tools/call depends on. Cross-checked against GHSA-4qq2-2j2x-x62c and the Network-AI GHSA-fj4g-2p96-q6m3 (same '0.0.0.0 + no auth' MCP pattern) plus Censys-reported mass MCP exposure. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/praisonai/GHSA-4qq2-2j2x-x62c/ • https://github.com/Jovancoding/Network-AI/security/advisories/GHSA-fj4g-2p96-q6m3 • https://www.anvilogic.com/threat-reports/mcp-server-auth-flaws
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: high — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate internet-facing Node app (Express/Next.js) intentionally bound to 0.0.0.0 on the host
  • Public load-balancer / uptime-monitor health probes hitting the Node port
  • Dev server bound to all interfaces in a DMZ or cloud VM with a public IP
  • Reverse-proxy host where the Node origin legitimately serves external clients
  • Container/k8s node with NodePort service exposing the app publicly by design
Suppression filter
| where LocalPort !in (80, 443)
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the LocalPort and process — confirm whether it is the PraisonAI MCPServer (check the app's startup code for server.start({port}) / node_modules/praisonai) versus a sanctioned web app. Pull the RemoteIPs and check for non-business geographies/ASNs. 2) Contain: if it is an MCP server, restrict the listener to 127.0.0.1 (or firewall the port to trusted CIDRs) and front it with an authenticating reverse proxy; block the offending source IPs at the edge. 3) Hunt: pivot the same DeviceId into UC2 (child-process spawns by that node PID) and UC3 (tools/call request bodies) to determine whether tools were actually invoked.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType == "InboundConnectionAccepted"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","node")
| where RemoteIPType == "Public"
| summarize FirstSeen=min(Timestamp), LastSeen=max(Timestamp), ConnCount=count(), RemoteIPs=make_set(RemoteIP, 50) by DeviceName, LocalPort, InitiatingProcessId, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by FirstSeen desc

PraisonAI MCP server (node) spawns shell/LOLBin child after external connection AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts post-exploitation of the unauthenticated tools/call dispatcher: registered MCP tools commonly wrap code execution and run with the server process privileges. This correlates a Node listener accepting an inbound public connection with that same node PID spawning a command interpreter/LOLBin within 5 minutes — i.e. an unauthenticated caller driving server-side tool execution. Rationale: Maps the article's Impact section — tool handlers 'run with the server process privileges' and an unauthenticated caller can 'drive the same actions' including 'code execution.' The Defender variant ties the inbound public reach (server PID owning the socket) to a child spawn by the same PID, turning a noisy node→shell pattern into a high-fidelity unauth-RCE signal. Corroborated by GHSA-6rmh-7xcm-cpxj (unauthenticated workflow execution) and PraisonAI unauth-RCE via tool_override.py. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-6rmh-7xcm-cpxj • https://advisories.gitlab.com/pypi/praisonai/CVE-2026-44334/
ATT&CKT1190 T1059
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • node-gyp / native module builds spawning cmd.exe during npm install
  • electron-builder or packaging tooling shelling out to powershell/cmd
  • CI/build agents running Node test runners that invoke shell scripts
  • pm2/forever/nodemon process managers spawning shells
  • Legitimately-registered MCP tools that are designed to run shell commands
Suppression filter
| where ProcessCommandLine !has_any ("node-gyp","electron-builder","npm-cli.js","node_modules\\.bin","prebuild-install")
Response runbook
1) Triage: inspect the child ProcessCommandLine and the correlated RemoteIP/LocalPort — distinguish a build-tool shell (node-gyp/npm) from attacker commands (recon, download, encoded payloads). Confirm the parent node process is an MCP server. 2) Contain: kill the node MCP process, isolate the host, and rotate any server-side credentials/API keys the MCP tools had access to (they ran under the server identity). 3) Hunt: enumerate all tools/resources the server registered, review UC3 for the tools/call bodies that triggered execution, and sweep other hosts running the praisonai package for the same node→shell pattern.
let Window = 5m;
let InboundReach = DeviceNetworkEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
    | where ActionType == "InboundConnectionAccepted"
    | where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","node")
    | where RemoteIPType == "Public"
    | project ConnTime=Timestamp, DeviceId, ServerPid=InitiatingProcessId, RemoteIP, LocalPort;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","node")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","bitsadmin.exe","bash.exe","sh.exe")
| join kind=inner InboundReach on DeviceId
| where InitiatingProcessId == ServerPid
| where Timestamp between (ConnTime .. ConnTime + Window)
| project ConnTime, SpawnTime=Timestamp, DelaySec=datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ConnTime), DeviceName, AccountName, RemoteIP, LocalPort, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by ConnTime desc
SecurityEvent

| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, ParentProcessName, NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

OAuth consent / suspicious app grant Actions on Objectives High

| project TimeGenerated, OperationName, User, AppName, AppId, Result
| order by TimeGenerated desc

npm PraisonAI AgentOS exposes unauthenticated agent listing and invocation

ATT&CKT1190 T1528

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] PraisonAI AgentOS Node server exposed to non-loopback on default port 8000
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Vulnerable PraisonAI npm package present (dist/os/agentos.js) on managed hosts
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Unauthenticated PraisonAI AgentOS API hits: GET /api/agents enumeration + POST /api/chat invocation · OAuth consent / suspicious app grant
PraisonAI AgentOS Node server exposed to non-loopback on default port 8000 AIExploitation Medium
Hunts for a node.exe process accepting inbound network connections from non-loopback/public source addresses on TCP 8000, the configuration the advisory describes when AgentOS keeps its default host '0.0.0.0' and is started with serve({ port: 8000 }). This surfaces PraisonAI AgentOS instances that are network-reachable without authentication. Rationale: Anchors on the advisory's named defaults: AgentOS binds host '0.0.0.0' with no auth and the PoV/serve example listens on port 8000 via a node process. Inbound acceptance from a non-loopback source on node:8000 is the network signature of an internet/LAN-reachable unauthenticated AgentOS. Port is configurable so this is hunting-tier; cross-checked against the sibling Python AgentOS advisory and Sysdig's observation of internet-wide scanning of these instances. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-892R-P3JQ-JP24 • https://webflow.sysdig.com/blog/cve-2026-44338-praisonai-authentication-bypass-in-under-4-hours-and-the-growing-trend-of-rapid-exploitation • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-6rmh-7xcm-cpxj
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate internal Node/Express dev servers (webpack-dev-server, http-server, json-server) that also default to port 8000
  • Authorized internal access to a PraisonAI AgentOS instance that has been correctly fronted by auth/reverse proxy
  • Vulnerability scanners / Nessus / internal pentest sweeps probing 8000 across the estate
  • Containerized microservices that legitimately publish 8000 to the host network
  • Health-check or load-balancer probes to the listener from internal monitoring subnets
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("praisonai", "agentos", "AgentOS") or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has "praisonai"
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the listening process is PraisonAI by inspecting InitiatingProcessCommandLine / the node working directory for a node_modules/praisonai path, and identify whether SampleSources are internal or public. 2) Contain: if reachable from untrusted networks, firewall TCP 8000 to loopback/management only and restart AgentOS bound to 127.0.0.1, or place an authenticating reverse proxy in front. 3) Hunt: pull web/proxy logs for the same host to see whether /api/agents or /api/chat were actually hit (UC2), and review agent tool/credential scope for signs of abuse.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType == "InboundConnectionAccepted"
| where LocalPort == 8000
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "node.exe"
| where RemoteIPType != "Loopback" and RemoteIP != "127.0.0.1" and RemoteIP != "::1"
| summarize Connections = count(), DistinctSources = dcount(RemoteIP), FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp), SampleSources = make_set(RemoteIP, 20) by DeviceName, LocalPort, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by LastSeen desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DstPortNumber == 8000
| where NetworkDirection =~ "Inbound"
| where SrcIpAddr !in ("127.0.0.1", "::1")
| where isnotempty(SrcIpAddr)
| summarize Connections = count(), DistinctSources = dcount(SrcIpAddr), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated), SampleSources = make_set(SrcIpAddr, 20) by DstHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| order by LastSeen desc
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:3 @DestinationPort:8000 @Image:(*\node.exe OR *\Node.exe OR *\NODE.EXE) -@SourceIp:(127.0.0.1 OR ::1)
#event_simpleName=NetworkConnectIP4 LocalPort=8000 ContextImageFileName=/node\.exe$/i
| ConnectionDirection=0
| NOT cidr("127.0.0.0/8", field=RemoteAddressIP4)
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, LocalPort, RemoteAddressIP4], function=count())

Unauthenticated PraisonAI AgentOS API hits: GET /api/agents enumeration + POST /api/chat invocation AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects HTTP access to the AgentOS routes /api/agents (discloses agent names, roles and instruction prefixes) and /api/chat (invokes the agent without credentials) returning 200. Same source IP touching both endpoints, or the documented scanner User-Agent, indicates the read-then-invoke exploitation flow the advisory describes. Rationale: Uses the advisory's exact route literals (apiPrefix default '/api' → /api/agents, /api/chat) and the 200-status fail-open behaviour (patched servers return 401). Requiring the same client to hit BOTH the enumeration and invocation routes encodes the read-then-invoke flow; the CVE-Detector/1.0 UA is the real scanner string Sysdig observed probing the identical endpoints on the sibling CVE-2026-44338. Defender XDR has no inbound-HTTP-request table, so defender_kql is intentionally empty; FDR/Datadog likewise don't ship web access logs. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-892R-P3JQ-JP24 • https://webflow.sysdig.com/blog/cve-2026-44338-praisonai-authentication-bypass-in-under-4-hours-and-the-growing-trend-of-rapid-exploitation • https://thehackernews.com/2026/05/praisonai-cve-2026-44338-auth-bypass.html
ATT&CKT1190 T1213
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developers or QA exercising /api/agents and /api/chat from a browser/Postman during testing
  • Application uptime monitors or synthetic transactions hitting both routes on a schedule
  • A correctly authenticated front-end legitimately calling both endpoints from one egress IP
  • Internal load testing tools generating GET+POST traffic to the AgentOS API
  • Security scanners other than CVE-Detector cataloguing the endpoints during an authorized assessment
Suppression filter
| where CIp !startswith "10." and CIp !startswith "192.168." and not (ipv4_is_in_range(CIp, "172.16.0.0/12"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: determine whether CIp is external/untrusted and whether the requests carried an Authorization header (check WAF/proxy header logs if available); a 200 on /api/chat from an external IP with no auth = confirmed unauthenticated invocation. 2) Contain: block the source IP at the proxy/WAF, take the AgentOS instance off untrusted networks, and rotate any credentials/tokens reachable by the invoked agent (env vars, downstream API keys). 3) Hunt: review the agent's tool/MCP/business-action logs for actions triggered in the request window, and pivot to UC1 to enumerate every exposed AgentOS listener in the estate.
AppServiceHTTPLogs
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where CsUriStem in~ ("/api/agents", "/api/chat")
| where ScStatus == 200
| summarize Requests = count(), Methods = make_set(CsMethod), Endpoints = make_set(CsUriStem), UserAgents = make_set(UserAgent, 20), FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated) by CIp, CsHost
| extend Scanner = iff(UserAgents has "CVE-Detector/1.0", "yes", "no")
| where (set_has_element(Endpoints, "/api/agents") and set_has_element(Endpoints, "/api/chat")) or Scanner == "yes"
| order by LastSeen desc

Vulnerable PraisonAI npm package present (dist/os/agentos.js) on managed hosts AIInstallation High
Asset-exposure hunt for the affected component: the file dist/os/agentos.js dropped under a node_modules/praisonai path (shipped in npm praisonai >=1.6.0,<=1.7.1), or an 'npm install praisonai' command. Identifies which hosts actually carry the unauthenticated AgentOS code so they can be hardened before exploitation. Rationale: Deterministic article artifact: the advisory names the affected component path src/praisonai-ts/.../os/agentos.ts which ships as dist/os/agentos.js inside the npm praisonai tarball (boundary: <=1.5.4 did not ship dist/os). Matching agentos.js under a praisonai folder pins the exact vulnerable code on disk with near-zero ambiguity. Affected range >=1.6.0,<=1.7.1 confirmed in the advisory; the unauthenticated-by-default behaviour corroborated by the sibling AgentOS advisory GHSA-892R-P3JQ-JP24. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-892R-P3JQ-JP24 • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-6rmh-7xcm-cpxj
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate, authorized PraisonAI deployment by an internal AI/ML team
  • CI/CD build agents that npm-install praisonai during pipeline runs
  • Developer workstations with the package in a local project's node_modules
  • Container image builds layering the package (file write seen at build time)
  • Security team intentionally staging the package for testing/reproduction
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the installed version (read package.json under the praisonai folder, or the InitiatingProcessCommandLine for an 'npm install praisonai@x'); flag any version in >=1.6.0,<=1.7.1. 2) Contain: ensure the AgentOS server is not started with the default host (bind 127.0.0.1), require an auth token, and firewall the listener; no fixed npm version existed at advisory time, so treat all installs as needing compensating controls. 3) Hunt: for each affected host, pivot to UC1 (is it listening on a non-loopback interface?) and UC2 (has anyone hit /api/agents or /api/chat?) to distinguish merely-installed from actively-exposed-and-probed.

PraisonAI codeMode sandbox-escape RCE: node process spawning OS shell / recon binaries AIExploitation Medium
Hunts a Node.js process running PraisonAI (praisonai-ts codeMode tool) spawning a shell, recon, or transfer binary. The codeMode sandbox is supposed to run JS in-process only; any child_process/execSync-spawned OS command from that node process is the direct manifestation of the Function-constructor blocklist bypass (GHSA-p69m-4f92-2v84). The PoC literally ran `id` via cp.execSync. Rationale: Anchors on the article's exact component (praisonai / codeMode) as the node parent plus a shell/recon child, because the sandbox is designed to run JS in-process only — a child OS process from it is the bypass executing. The PoC's `id` recon is included. Cross-checked GHSA-vmmj-pfw7-fjwp and CVE-2026-40158 which confirm child_process recovery via the Function constructor. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/praisonai/GHSA-vmmj-pfw7-fjwp/ • https://www.sentinelone.com/vulnerability-database/cve-2026-40158/ • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-p69m-4f92-2v84
ATT&CKT1059.007 T1059.004 T1033
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A PraisonAI deployment whose node command line legitimately invokes the python_tools subprocess sandbox (spawns python3)
  • Build/CI tooling running under the same node process tree (npm scripts, node-gyp, webpack) shelling out
  • Process managers like pm2/nodemon restarting and spawning sh
  • Container entrypoint scripts where node is PID 1 and forks a shell for health checks
  • Developer running node REPL with child_process during local testing
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has_any ("npm","node-gyp","yarn","webpack","install","healthcheck") and not (FileName =~ "python3" and ProcessCommandLine has "_execute_code_sandboxed")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the child ChildCmd and parent ParentCmd — confirm the parent node is actually running praisonai (check folder/cmdline) and the child is not a build/health-check script. Note the OS command run (id/whoami/curl indicate hands-on-keyboard). 2) Contain: isolate the host/container, kill the node process tree, and rotate any API keys/tokens/secrets present in that process's environment (env-var exfil is the stated impact). 3) Hunt: pivot on the same host/aid for outbound egress from node (UC3), for written persistence files, and confirm the praisonai version — upgrade to >=1.7.2.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","node.exe")
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "praisonai" or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has "praisonai"
| where not(InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm","node-gyp","yarn","webpack","install"))
| where FileName in~ ("sh","bash","dash","zsh","cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh","id","whoami","uname","hostname","curl","wget","nc","ncat","python3")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          ParentImage = InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          ParentCmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          ChildProcess = FileName, ChildCmd = ProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where EventType == "ProcessCreated"
| where ActingProcessName has "node"
| where ActingProcessCommandLine has "praisonai" or ActingProcessName has "praisonai"
| where not(ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("npm","node-gyp","yarn","webpack"))
| where TargetProcessName has_any ("/bin/sh","/bin/bash","/bin/dash","/usr/bin/zsh","cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh","/usr/bin/id","whoami","uname","hostname","curl","wget","ncat")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: PraisonAI codeMode Sandbox Escape - Node Spawning OS Command Interpreter
id: 4f3c2a1b-8d6e-4c5a-9b2f-1e7a6c0d9b34
status: experimental
description: Detects a Node.js process spawning a shell or recon binary, indicating exploitation of the PraisonAI codeMode Function-constructor sandbox escape (GHSA-p69m-4f92-2v84 / GHSA-vmmj-pfw7-fjwp / CVE-2026-40158).
references:
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-p69m-4f92-2v84
  - https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/praisonai/GHSA-vmmj-pfw7-fjwp/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/20
tags:
  - attack.execution
  - attack.t1059.007
  - attack.t1059.004
logsource:
  category: process_creation
detection:
  selection_parent:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '/node'
      - '\\node.exe'
  selection_child:
    Image|endswith:
      - '/sh'
      - '/bash'
      - '/dash'
      - '\\cmd.exe'
      - '\\powershell.exe'
      - '/id'
      - '/whoami'
      - '/curl'
      - '/wget'
  condition: selection_parent and selection_child
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @ParentImage:(*\\node.exe OR *\\Node.exe OR *\\NODE.EXE) @ParentCommandLine:*praisonai* @Image:(*\\cmd.exe OR *\\powershell.exe OR *\\PowerShell.exe OR *\\pwsh.exe OR *\\curl.exe OR *\\wget.exe)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ParentBaseFileName=/^node(\.exe)?$/i
| ImageFileName=/(sh|bash|dash|cmd\.exe|powershell\.exe|pwsh|id|whoami|uname|curl|wget|ncat)$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ImageFileName, CommandLine], function=count())
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats summariesonly=true count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.parent_process_name="node" OR Processes.parent_process_name="node.exe") (Processes.process_name IN ("sh","bash","dash","zsh","cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh","id","whoami","uname","curl","wget","ncat")) by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.parent_process_name Processes.parent_process Processes.process_name Processes.process | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | search (parent_process="*praisonai*" OR parent_process="*node_modules*praisonai*") AND parent_process!="*npm*" AND parent_process!="*node-gyp*" | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes where (Processes.parent_process_name="node" OR Processes.parent_process_name="node.exe") (Processes.process_name IN ("sh","bash","dash","zsh","cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh","id","whoami","uname","curl","wget","ncat")) by Processes.dest Processes.user Processes.parent_process_name Processes.parent_process Processes.process_name Processes.process | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)` | search (parent_process="*praisonai*" OR parent_process="*node_modules*praisonai*") AND parent_process!="*npm*" AND parent_process!="*node-gyp*" | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
Vulnerable PraisonAI codeMode component present on host (code-mode.ts/js under praisonai) AIReconnaissance High
Asset-discovery hunt for the vulnerable file path named in the advisory — src/praisonai-ts/src/tools/builtins/code-mode.ts (and its compiled code-mode.js) under any praisonai directory. Surfaces every host/container carrying the exploitable codeMode tool so they can be confirmed against the patched 1.7.2 release. Rationale: Uses the advisory's exact vulnerable file path (src/praisonai-ts/src/tools/builtins/code-mode.ts) plus the compiled .js form to enumerate exposure. High confidence the file maps to the affected component; version confirmation (vs patched 1.7.2 from GHSA-vmmj-pfw7-fjwp) separates vulnerable from fixed. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/praisonai/GHSA-vmmj-pfw7-fjwp/ • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-p69m-4f92-2v84
ATT&CKT1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Hosts running the PATCHED praisonai >=1.7.2 which still ships a code-mode file (fixed, not vulnerable) — verify package.json/version
  • Source-code mirrors, package caches, or git clones of praisonai-ts that are not actually executed
  • Security researchers' local checkout of the advisory PoC
  • Backup/restore images surfacing historical node_modules
  • Container image-build layers scanned by the EDR during CI
Response runbook
1) Triage: for each hit, resolve the installed praisonai version (read package.json near the FolderPath or query the package manager) and whether codeMode is enabled for any agent. 2) Contain: on confirmed vulnerable (1.4.0–1.7.1) production hosts, disable the codeMode tool and/or upgrade to >=1.7.2; restrict the node process's egress and secrets exposure in the interim. 3) Hunt: correlate the host against UC1 (node spawning shells) and UC3 (node novel egress) to determine whether the vuln was merely present or actually exploited.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FolderPath has "praisonai"
| where FileName in~ ("code-mode.ts","code-mode.js")
| summarize FirstSeen=min(Timestamp), LastSeen=max(Timestamp), arg_max(Timestamp, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessAccountName) by DeviceName, FolderPath, FileName
| order by LastSeen desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetFilePath has "praisonai"
| where TargetFileName in~ ("code-mode.ts","code-mode.js")
| summarize FirstSeen=min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen=max(TimeGenerated) by DvcHostname, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, ActingProcessName
| order by LastSeen desc
title: PraisonAI Vulnerable codeMode File Present (GHSA-p69m-4f92-2v84)
id: b7e1d4c2-3a9f-4d61-8c0e-2f5b9a7c1e88
status: experimental
description: Flags the presence/write of the vulnerable PraisonAI codeMode tool file (code-mode.ts/js) under a praisonai directory. Confirm package version against patched 1.7.2.
references:
  - https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-p69m-4f92-2v84

author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/20

  - attack.execution

  category: file_event
detection:
  selection:
    TargetFilename|contains: 'praisonai'
    TargetFilename|endswith:

source:windows.sysmon @EventID:11 @TargetFilename:(*praisonai*code-mode.ts OR *praisonai*code-mode.js OR *PraisonAI*code-mode.ts OR *PraisonAI*code-mode.js)
#event_simpleName=FileWritten
| TargetFileName=/praisonai.*code-mode\.(ts|js)$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, TargetFileName], function=count())

| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem where Filesystem.file_path="*praisonai*" (Filesystem.file_name="code-mode.ts" OR Filesystem.file_name="code-mode.js") by Filesystem.dest Filesystem.file_path Filesystem.file_name | `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)` | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime)
PraisonAI node process novel outbound egress (post-escape secret/API-key exfiltration or C2) AIActions on Objectives Low
Hunts a node process running PraisonAI making outbound connections to destinations outside the expected set of LLM/API/registry endpoints. After the codeMode escape, the stated impact is exfiltrating environment variables (API keys, tokens) and installing backdoors — both produce egress from the node process to attacker infrastructure not seen in a normal agent's API traffic. Rationale: Anchors on the praisonai node process (so it's article-specific, not generic node egress) and the advisory's named impact of env-var/API-key exfiltration and backdoor install. Excludes the normal LLM/API/registry endpoints an agent legitimately calls so residual hits are candidate exfil/C2. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/npm/praisonai/GHSA-vmmj-pfw7-fjwp/ • https://github.com/advisories/GHSA-p69m-4f92-2v84
ATT&CKT1041 T1552.001 T1059.007
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: high — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • The agent legitimately calling a self-hosted or less-common LLM/inference endpoint not in the allowlist (e.g. private Ollama host, Together/Groq/Bedrock endpoints)
  • Tool/plugin functionality that fetches external web content as part of normal agent tasks
  • Telemetry/APM SDKs bundled in the node app beaconing to vendor SaaS
  • Package/dependency fetches via a private registry mirror
  • CDN/object-storage pulls for model or asset downloads
Suppression filter
| where RemoteUrl !endswith ".bedrock.amazonaws.com" and RemoteUrl !has_any ("together.ai","groq.com","datadoghq.com","sentry.io")
Response runbook
1) Triage: review the destination(s) — resolve WHOIS/reputation and whether they are known LLM/SaaS infra; check timing against any UC1 shell-spawn hit on the same host. 2) Contain: if the destination is unrecognized, block it at egress, isolate the host/container, and immediately rotate all secrets in the node process environment (the named impact is API-key/token theft). 3) Hunt: inspect the node process tree for child shells (UC1) and for newly written startup/backdoor files; expand the allowlist for confirmed-benign endpoints to tune the hunt.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","node.exe")
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "praisonai" or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has "praisonai"
| where RemoteIPType == "Public"
| where not(RemoteUrl has_any ("api.openai.com","api.anthropic.com","openai.azure.com","googleapis.com","api.cohere.ai","api.mistral.ai","huggingface.co","ollama.com","registry.npmjs.org"))
| summarize FirstSeen=min(Timestamp), ConnCount=count(), Ports=make_set(RemotePort,10) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteIP, RemoteUrl
| order by FirstSeen desc

#event_simpleName=DnsRequest

RMM tool installed by non-IT user — remote-access utility for hands-on-keyboard Installation High
ConnectWise / AnyDesk / TeamViewer / ScreenConnect / Atera installed outside IT change windows = common tradecraft for ransomware affiliates and IT-helpdesk impersonators.
ATT&CKT1219
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName in~ ("AnyDesk.exe","TeamViewer.exe","TeamViewer_Service.exe",
        "ScreenConnect.ClientService.exe","ConnectWiseControl.ClientService.exe",
        "atera_agent.exe","SplashtopStreamer.exe","RustDesk.exe","NinjaOne.exe")
   or FileName matches regex @"(?i)kaseya.*\.exe"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\AnyDesk.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\TeamViewer.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\TeamViewer_Service.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\ScreenConnect.ClientService.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\ConnectWiseControl.ClientService.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\atera_agent.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\SplashtopStreamer.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\RustDesk.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\NinjaOne.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName,
          CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Remote-Management (RMM) Tool Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d0c
status: experimental
description: |
    AnyDesk / TeamViewer / ScreenConnect / Atera / Splashtop /
    RustDesk — RMM tools attackers commonly install for hands-on-
    keyboard access. Filter against the IT estate's known tool.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1219/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1219
    - attack.command-and-control
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\AnyDesk.exe'
            - '\TeamViewer.exe'
            - '\TeamViewer_Service.exe'
            - '\ScreenConnect.ClientService.exe'
            - '\ConnectWiseControl.ClientService.exe'
            - '\atera_agent.exe'
            - '\SplashtopStreamer.exe'
            - '\RustDesk.exe'
            - '\NinjaOne.exe'
    condition: selection
falsepositives:
    - IT-approved RMM in use across the estate. Maintain a known-IT-
      tool exclusion list per environment.
level: high

       Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_path
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| `security_content_ctime(firstTime)`
| append [
| tstats summariesonly=false count
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("created","modified")
      AND (Filesystem.file_name IN ("node.js","python_tools.py"))
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, Filesystem.process_name,
       Filesystem.file_path, Filesystem.file_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
]
